=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string s and an integer k.
In one operation, you can replace the character at any position with the next or previous letter in the alphabet (wrapping around so that 'a' is after 'z'). For example, replacing 'a' with the next letter results in 'b', and replacing 'a' with the previous letter results in 'z'. Similarly, replacing 'z' with the next letter results in 'a', and replacing 'z' with the previous letter results in 'y'.
Return the length of the longest palindromic subsequence of s that can be obtained after performing at most k operations.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "abced", k = 2
Output: 3
Explanation:

Replace s[1] with the next letter, and s becomes "acced".
Replace s[4] with the previous letter, and s becomes "accec".

The subsequence "ccc" forms a palindrome of length 3, which is the maximum.

Example 2:

Input: s = "aaazzz", k = 4
Output: 6
Explanation:

Replace s[0] with the previous letter, and s becomes "zaazzz".
Replace s[4] with the next letter, and s becomes "zaazaz".
Replace s[3] with the next letter, and s becomes "zaaaaz".

The entire string forms a palindrome of length 6.

 
Constraints:

1 <= s.length <= 200
1 <= k <= 200
s consists of only lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def longestPalindromicSubsequence(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's think of DP recurrence proof:
Let f(l,r,c) = max length of palindromic subsequence obtainable from substring s[l..r] with at most c operations.
For any optimal subsequence in s[l..r]:
- If it doesn't use l, it's in s[l+1..r] cost <=c, length <= f(l+1,r,c).
- If it doesn't use r, similarly <= f(l,r-1,c).
- If it uses both l and r as outermost chars of the palindrome (they must be paired), then in final palindrome these two positions have same char. To minimize operations for this pair, choose a char on shortest path between original s[l] and s[r], cost d(s[l],s[r]); remaining c-d operations for inner subsequence between l+1..r-1. Length = 2 + f(l+1,r-1,c-d). Actually if original chars equal d=0. If d > c, cannot use both. So recurrence.
Need include if optimal uses l but not r or vice versa covered by skip. If uses both but they are not necessarily adjacent in subsequence? In a palindrome, if l and r are both used, they are outermost because l is leftmost position and r rightmost position in substring. Any subsequence using l and r will have l as first selected and r as last selected, so they must be equal. Good.

Potential code:

class Solution:
    def longestPalindromicSubsequence(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        K = k
        vals = [ord(ch) - 97 for ch in s]
        dist = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
        for i in range(n):
            vi = vals[i]
            for j in range(n):
                d = abs(vi - vals[j])
                if d > 13:
                    d = 26 - d
                dist[i][j] = d

        K1 = K + 1
        prev2 = [[0] * K1 for _ in range(n + 1)]  # length 0
        prev1 = [[1] * K1 for _ in range(n)]      # length 1

        for length in range(2, n + 1):
            curr = [None] * (n - length + 1)
            for l in range(n - length + 1):
                r = l + length - 1
                d = dist[l][r]
                skip_l = prev1[l + 1]
                skip_r = prev1[l]
                inner = prev2[l + 1]
                arr = [0] * K1
                # local variables for speed
                if d <= K:
                    # loop with match
                    for c in range(K1):
                        best = skip_l[c]
                        if skip_r[c] > best:
                            best = skip_r[c]
                        if c >= d:
                            val = inner[c - d] + 2
                            if val > best:
                                best = val
                        arr[c] = best
                else:
                    # no match possible within budget
                    for c in range(K1):
                        best = skip_l[c]
                        if skip_r[c] > best:
                            best = skip_r[c]
                        arr[c] = best
                curr[l] = arr
            prev2, prev1 = prev1, curr

        return prev1[0][K]

Before final, let's maybe think about if there is an even more subtle issue: The operation can replace a character with next or previous letter. If we want to transform x to y, the number of operations is the length of a path on the cycle. The shortest path distance is min(|x-y|, 26-|x-y|). But if we choose final char c for pair x,y, cost = d(x,c)+d(y,c). We said min = d(x,y). Let's prove for cycle metric: d is a metric, so d(x,c)+d(y,c) >= d(x,y). Equality if c lies on a shortest path between x and y. Does such c always exist? Yes, take c=x: d(x,x)+d(y,x)=d(x,y). Wait! If c=x, cost = 0 + d(x,y) = d(x,y). Oh! Even simpler: choose final char equal to s[l] (original left char), then change right char to left char cost d(x,y). Or choose final char equal to s[r]. So pair cost <= d(x,y). Triangle gives >=. Thus min = d. Great. We don't even need shortest path intermediate. This is important: To make two chars equal, we can just change one of them to the other. Is that always minimal? Yes by triangle inequality. So pair cost = distance. Good. This also means no need to consider final char different from both; changing both could be same total but not less. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
.

```python
class Solution:
    def longestPalindromicSubsequence(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        K = k
        vals = [ord(ch) - 97 for ch in s]

        dist = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(n):
                d = abs(vals[i] - vals[j])
                dist[i][j] = min(d, 26 - d)

        K1 = K + 1

        # prev2: intervals of length 0, prev1: intervals of length 1
        prev2 = [[0] * K1 for _ in range(n + 1)]
        prev1 = [[1] * K1 for _ in range(n)]

        for length in range(2, n + 1):
            curr = [None] * (n - length + 1)

            for l in range(n - length + 1):
                r = l + length - 1
                d = dist[l][r]

                skip_l = prev1[l + 1]
                skip_r = prev1[l]
                inner = prev2[l + 1]

                arr = [0] * K1

                if d <= K:
                    for c in range(K1):
                        best = skip_l[c]
                        if skip_r[c] > best:
                            best = skip_r[c]
                        if c >= d:
                            val = inner[c - d] + 2
                            if val > best:
                                best = val
                        arr[c] = best
                else:
                    for c in range(K1):
                        best = skip_l[c]
                        if skip_r[c] > best:
                            best = skip_r[c]
                        arr[c] = best

                curr[l] = arr

            prev2, prev1 = prev1, curr

        return prev1[0][K]
```
